 <p align="center"> <img src="doc/logo.png" width="640" alt="Llama 3.1 8B Instruct quantization benchmark across bits per weight"> </p> [Installation](#installation) · [Supported models](#architecture-support) · [Examples](#examples) · [Multi-node](#multi-node-inference) · [Quantization](#exl3-quantization) · [Community](#community) ExLlamaV3 is an inference library for running local LLMs on modern consumer GPUs, with flexible quantization and parallel inference. - **Quantization** - [EXL3](#exl3-quantization), based on QTIP, plus 2–8 bit cache quantization. - **Parallel inference** - Flexible tensor-parallel and expert-parallel inference for consumer hardware setups. - **CPU offloading** - Allows large MoE models to run with limited GPU resources. AVX2 and AVX512 support. - **Generation** - Continuous, dynamic batching, speculative decoding, multimodal support. - **Integrations** - Broad [HF model support](#architecture-support), a [Transformers plugin](examples/transformers_integration.py), and an OpenAI-compatible API via [TabbyAPI](https://github.com/theroyallab/tabbyAPI/). > [!TIP] > **Looking for a server?** [TabbyAPI](https://github.com/theroyallab/tabbyAPI/) is the official and recommended backend server. It provides an OpenAI-compatible API for local or remote inference, HF model downloading, embedding model support, and HF Jinja2 chat templates. Its startup script manages and installs prerequisites to help you get started. <p align="center"> <img src="doc/qb_kld.png" width="640" alt="Llama 3.1 8B Instruct quantization benchmark across bits per weight"> </p> ## Installation Start by making sure you have the appropriate version of [PyTorch](https://pytorch.org/get-started/locally/) installed (CUDA 12.4 or later) since the Torch dependency is not automatically handled by `pip`. Then pick a method below: ### Prebuilt wheel · recommended Pick a wheel from the [releases page](https://github.com/turboderp-org/exllamav3/releases), then e.g.: ```sh pip install https://github.com/turboderp-org/exllamav3/releases/download/v0.0.6/exllamav3-0.0.6+cu128.torch2.8.0-cp313-cp313-linux_x86_64.whl ``` ### Prebuilt wheels from this fork This fork publishes wheels for every release tag on its own [releases page](https://github.com/vcruz305/exllamav3/releases) - no CUDA toolkit, compiler or arch list on your side. Assets are named ``` exllamav3-<version>+cu<CUDA>.torch<torch>-cp<py>-cp<py>-<platform>.whl ``` so `exllamav3-1.5.1.post1+cu128.torch2.11.0-cp312-cp312-linux_x86_64.whl` is the CUDA 12.8 build for Python 3.12 with torch 2.11.0, and the same row on Windows ends in `win_amd64`. Pick the row matching the `torch`/CUDA pair you want; the wheel requires that exact `torch` version (see the caveat below). Linux/macOS: ```sh pip install https://github.com/vcruz305/exllamav3/releases/download/v1.5.1.post1/exllamav3-1.5.1.post1+cu128.torch2.11.0-cp312-cp312-linux_x86_64.whl ``` Windows (quote the URL - PowerShell treats `+` and `&` specially): ```powershell pip install "https://github.com/vcruz305/exllamav3/releases/download/v1.5.1.post1/exllamav3-1.5.1.post1+cu128.torch2.11.0-cp312-cp312-win_amd64.whl" ``` Rows built per release: Python 3.10-3.14 x torch 2.8.0-2.13.0 x CUDA 12.8 (`cu128`) and 13.2 (`cu132`), for Linux and Windows. Not every cell exists - torch 2.12/2.13 only ship on the CUDA 13.2 row, and torch 2.11 has no Python 3.10/3.11 wheels - so the release page is the list of what is actually available. - **No local build, no arch guessing.** Every wheel carries the fat CUDA arch list `8.0 8.6 8.9 9.0 10.0 12.0+PTX`: Ampere through Blackwell, including `sm_120` natively and newer parts such as GB10 (`sm_121`) through the `12.0+PTX` JIT entry. At runtime a wheel only needs a CUDA *driver*; the toolkit it was built against does not have to be installed locally. - **Nightly channel.** The rolling pre-release tagged [`nightly`](https://github.com/vcruz305/exllamav3/releases/tag/nightly) holds one Linux/CUDA 12.8 wheel built from the tip of `master`, with the same fat arch list. Its single asset is replaced on every push, so install from the URL rather than a saved copy: ```sh pip install --force-reinstall https://github.com/vcruz305/exllamav3/releases/download/nightly/exllamav3-1.5.1.post1+cu128.torch2.11.0-cp312-cp312-linux_x86_64.whl ``` - **Caveat - the package name is `exllamav3`.** These wheels are not a separate distribution, so installing one *replaces* an upstream `exllamav3` (PyPI or source) install in that environment instead of sitting next to it. Each wheel also pins its exact `torch` version as a requirement, so pip will install or downgrade `torch` to match it; to keep the torch you already have, install torch first and then the wheel with `--no-deps`. A virtualenv per fork build is the simple way to avoid surprises. ### Install from PyPI ```sh pip install exllamav3 ``` Note that the PyPI package does not contain a prebuilt extension and requires the CUDA toolkit and build prerequisites (i.e. VS Build Tools on Windows, gcc on Linux, `python-dev` headers etc.). ### Build from source <details> <summary>Source installation with uv or pip</summary> `exllamav3` declares a minimum `torch` version (>= 2.6.0) and CUDA version (>= 12.4), but beyond that the user is free to select a version of `torch` that is compatible with their environment. `torch` can be installed in three ways (from least to most effort): 1. **with `uv`, setting only `--extra cuXXX`** installs `torch` automatically with the specified CUDA version, `torch` version is selected by `uv` from compatible versions in the specific index associated with the chosen CUDA version (options 1 and 2) 2. **with `uv`, creating a thin project that depends on `exllamav3[cuXXX]` and pins a specific `torch` version** — like (1) but `torch` is pinned in the thin project's `pyproject.toml`, see [pinning a specific PyTorch version (optional)](#pinning-a-specific-pytorch-version-optional) for details 3. Manually with `uv pip` or `pip` (options 3 and 4) The flavor extras (`--extra`) are `cu124`, `cu126`, `cu128`, `cu129`, `cu130`, and `cu132` — pick the one matching your installed CUDA build. Both `uv sync` and `pip install .` build the package in an isolated environment where your `torch` is not visible, so they install the extension sources and compile them at first import (JIT, a few minutes once per torch version). For a precompiled install run `pip install --no-build-isolation .` in an environment that already has `torch`, or use the release wheels. Selecting a flavor installs the matching CUDA build of `torch`. **Option 1 — Working in the cloned repo directly (`uv sync`):** ```sh git clone https://github.com/turboderp-org/exllamav3 cd exllamav3 # (Optional) switch to dev branch for latest in-progress features git checkout dev uv venv uv sync --extra cu130 # add --extra examples and/or --extra eval for those extra dependencies ``` **Option 2 — Using `exllamav3` as a dependency from another project (`uv add`):** ```sh # `uv add` works inside an existing project (a directory with a pyproject.toml). # `uv init` creates one if you're starting a new project, if integrating into # an existing project skip `uv init`. uv init my-project cd my-project # local checkout uv add 'path/to/exllamav3[cu130]' # non-editable uv add 'path/to/exllamav3[cu130]' --editable # editable # straight from GitHub uv add 'git+https://github.com/turboderp-org/exllamav3.git[cu130]' # default branch uv add 'git+https://github.com/turboderp-org/exllamav3.git[cu130]' --branch dev # specific branch ``` **Option 3 — Bring your own `torch` and let `uv` pick the backend automatically:** ```sh uv venv # or: uv venv --python-preference only-managed source .venv/bin/activate uv pip install torch --torch-backend=auto uv pip install . ``` `--torch-backend=auto` inspects your system and installs the matching PyTorch CUDA build; see [Automatic backend selection](https://docs.astral.sh/uv/guides/integration/pytorch/#automatic-backend-selection). **Option 4 — With `pip`:** On Windows, you also need the `triton-windows` package (declared as a dependency in `pyproject.toml`); the attention, cache and recurrent kernels are Triton and ExLlamaV3 does not import without it. ```sh # install a CUDA-enabled torch first so it matches your setup, e.g.: pip install torch --index-url https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu128 pip install . ``` </details> <details> <summary>Pinning a specific PyTorch version (optional)</summary> #### Pinning a specific PyTorch version (optional) The flavor extra picks the *index*, but by default torch resolves to the latest version on that index that satisfies `>=2.6.0`. To pin a specific torch version while developing on `exllamav3`, create a **"thin" project** that consumes your local checkout as an editable install and declares the exact `torch` version itself. This keeps the pin out of the `exllamav3` pyproject, so you can change the torch version freely without touching the repo. ``` my-exllamav3-dev/ # thin project (uv init) ├── pyproject.toml └── src/ # package sources (auto-generated) ``` In `pyproject.toml`: ```toml [project] name = "my-exllamav3-dev" version = "0.1.0" description = "Dev environment for exllamav3" requires-python = ">=3.10.11" dependencies = [ "exllamav3[cu130]", # select correct CUDA version "torch==2.13.0", # pin the exact torch version you need ] [tool.uv.sources] exllamav3 = { path = "../exllamav3", editable = true } ``` Adjust `../exllamav3` to point at your local checkout, then a plain `uv sync` sets up an environment with the correct PyTorch index (routed via the `cuXXX` extra), the pinned version of `torch` from that index (as long as it exists), and an editable install of `exllamav3` so code changes apply immediately. Switch CUDA flavors by changing the extra (`exllamav3[cu124]`, `exllamav3[cu128]`, …) and/or the torch pin in the thin project. Or, if you're installing torch manually with `uv pip install torch` (e.g. as in Option 3 above), specify the version directly, e.g. `uv pip install "torch==2.11.0" --torch-backend=auto`. </details> After installing with one of the options above, you should be able to run the conversion, eval and example scripts from the main repo directory, e.g., `uv run python convert.py -i ...` or, for manual installations once the venv is active, `python convert.py -i ...` **Build environment variables** - `MAX_JOBS`: by default ninja may launch too many processes and run out of system memory for compilation. Set this to a reasonable value like 4 in that case. - `EXLLAMA_NOCOMPILE`: set to install the library without compiling the C++/CUDA extension. Torch will build/load it at runtime instead. - `EXLLAMA_EXT_LINEINFO`, `EXLLAMA_EXT_COMPRESS`: see [doc/env_vars.md](doc/env_vars.md). ## Examples A number of example scripts are provided to showcase the features of the backend and generator. For instance, a versatile CLI chatbot: <p align="center"> <img src="doc/chatpy.png" width="640" alt="Llama 3.1 8B Instruct quantization benchmark across bits per weight"> </p> ```sh python examples/chat.py -m <input_dir> -mode <prompt_mode> # Wealth of options python examples/chat.py -h ``` ## Multi-node inference [`examples/multinode_pipeline.py`](examples/multinode_pipeline.py) runs **sequential layer-split pipeline parallelism (PP)** across hosts, with TCP (`NetEndpoint`) or `torch.distributed` point-to-point transport (`NcclEndpoint`, NCCL/RoCE on CUDA). Each rank loads only its assigned layers. This is not the library's single-host TP worker system, an overlapped pipeline scheduler, or a packaged cross-host TP/CP server. The four-Spark GLM work recovered pinned-buffer lifetime fixes, byte-safe TCP staging, NCCL p2p, DSA-safe layer splits and the sm_121 cooperative mixed-K decode default; these are included here. See the [multi-node guide](doc/multinode.md) for build/runtime identity, generic four-rank launch commands, memory and precision rules, historical short-context validation, and what remains outside this PR. Read the [transport and driver limits](doc/multinode_pipeline_limits.md) before deployment: trusted fabric only, finite frame/I/O budgets, numeric fabric IPs for hard TCP connect deadlines, and automatic recurrent-state handling for GLM-5.3-Flash. Other recurrent/hybrid families remain fail-closed in the manual PP loop. ## Architecture support | Model family | HF architecture | Multimodal | Notes | For the **K2-Horizon MoVA 6.50bpw** checkpoint at its pinned revision, see the [missing routing-bias recovery guide](doc/k2-horizon-bias-overlay.md). This is not a blanket claim of support for other K2 quant revisions. ## Conversion To convert a model to EXL3 format, use: ```sh # Convert model python convert.py -i <input_dir> -o <output_dir> -w <working_dir> -b <bitrate> # Resume an interrupted quant job python convert.py -w <working_dir> -r # More options python convert.py -h ``` The working directory is temporary storage for state checkpoints and for storing quantized tensors until the converted model can be compiled. It should have enough free space to store an entire copy of the output model. ### Conversion speed / multi-GPU Calibration forwards default to `-cbs auto` (32 rows per forward, capped by the calibration row count) and `-cdn auto` (one calibration device when batching; otherwise all selected devices). An out-of-memory error on a batched forward releases cached CUDA memory, halves that worker's batch, and retries the same rows, down to the original one-row path. Quantization continues to use every device supplied with `-d`. Set `-cbs N` to choose the calibration rows per forward and `-cdn N` to use the first N devices for calibration. These settings are momentary and may be changed on `-r` resume. Restore the pre-batching behavior with `-cbs 1 -cdn 0`. Measured on one MoE layer of Qwen3.5 35B-A3B, 256 experts, 256 calibration rows: | Configuration | Capture time | | --- | ---: | | Previous path, 2 devices | 114 s | | Previous path, 1 device | 36 s | | `-cbs 16`, 2 devices | 27 s | | `-cbs 16 -cdn 1` | 21 s | | `-cbs 32 -cdn 1` | 16 s | On the measured 8x B300, 512-row run, capture per layer changed from 330 s to 23 s; total per-layer time changed from about 520 s to 219 s. These are measurements from the fork before this defaults change. An A/B comparison on embedding and layer 0 (64 rows, same recipe, `-sh -v -cb mul1`) compared 846 tensors: median per-tensor proxy relative difference was 0 at printed precision; mean proxy was 1.02814e-4 vs 1.02826e-4, and layer RFN/SQNR were 0.019624/36.380 vs 0.019572/36.389. Differences were from fp32 summation order. Both options are resume-safe and are not written to `args.json`. See the [conversion guide](doc/convert.md) for more information, or the [self-calibration guide](doc/optimize.md). ## EXL3 quantization EXL3 quantization is a streamlined variant of [**QTIP**](https://github.com/Cornell-RelaxML/qtip) from Cornell RelaxML. It aims to make SOTA quantization available to users on consumer hardware. The conversion process is designed to be simple and efficient and requires only an input model (in HF format) and a target bitrate. By computing Hessians on the fly and thanks to a fused Viterbi kernel, the quantizer can convert a model in a single step, taking a couple of minutes for smaller models, up to a few hours for larger ones (70B+) on a single high-end consumer GPU (see the [conversion guide](doc/convert.md)). For more information, see the [**QTIP**](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.11235) and [**QuIP#**](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.04396) papers, as well as this [excellent writeup](https://www.together.ai/blog/even-better-even-faster-quantized-llms-with-qtip) on **QTIP** from together.ai. ## Community You are always welcome to join the [ExLlama discord server](https://discord.gg/NSFwVuCjRq) ←🎮 ### 🤗 Models on Hugging Face Browse the [EXL3 model collection](https://huggingface.co/collections/turboderp/exl3-models-67f2dfe530f05cb9f596d21a) for quantized models. Also shout out to the following lovely people: - [ArtusDev](https://huggingface.co/ArtusDev) - [MikeRoz](https://huggingface.co/MikeRoz) - [MetaphoricalCode](https://huggingface.co/MetaphoricalCode) - [Ready.Art](https://huggingface.co/ReadyArt) - [isogen](https://huggingface.co/isogen/models) ## Acknowledgements This project owes its existence to a wonderful community of FOSS developers and some very generous supporters (🐈❤️!) The following projects in particular deserve a special mention: - [TabbyAPI](https://github.com/theroyallab/tabbyAPI/) - [PyTorch](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch) - [FlashAttention](https://github.com/Dao-AILab/flash-attention) - [QTIP](https://github.com/Cornell-RelaxML/qtip) - [Transformers](https://github.com/huggingface/transformers) - [Marlin](https://github.com/IST-DASLab/marlin) - [Flash Linear Attention](https://github.com/fla-org/flash-linear-attention) (chunked linear-attention prefill kernels, vendored under `exllamav3/vendor/fla`) <p align="center"> <img src="doc/cat.png" width="40" alt=""> </p> # EXL3 models on an Intel Arc Pro B70 Serving EXL3 models on one **Intel Arc Pro B70** (Battlemage, 32 GB) in a desktop with 62 GB of RAM and a PCIe 4 NVMe SSD, including mixture-of-experts models far larger than the card: the experts a workload uses most stay on the card, more sit in pinned RAM, and the rest come from an NVMe store; the router is nudged towards experts that are close by, at a measured cost in quality. | Model | Size | Decode, 1 user | Prompt | Host RAM | Server | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | [GLM-5.3-Flash](glm/README.md) 2.51 bpw EXL3 | ~105 GB | ~15-18 tok/s | 8K tokens in 14.4 s | ~30 GB | exllamav3 port (`glm/`) | | [Qwen3.8-Flash-Next](flashnext/README.md) 3.05 bpw EXL3 | 68 GB | 41.7 tok/s (68 total for 2 users, ~95 for 4) | 18K tokens in 15.9 s | ~15 GB | SGLang + trellis-serve + patch | | [Gemma 4 31B](gemma/README.md) 4.0 bpw EXL3 (dense) | 17 GB | 36-45 tok/s with its drafter (135 total for 4) | 12K tokens in 6.7 s; context ~46K, ~107K packed | small | SGLang + trellis-serve + patch | No Docker and no root: both servers run inside an unpacked copy of the `lmsysorg/sglang` XPU image (PyTorch 2.13 XPU, Triton XPU, oneAPI 2026.0) through bubblewrap. ## Running it You need Linux with the `xe` kernel driver, `bwrap` (bubblewrap), `git` and `python3` on the host, ~25 GB for the image, the model, and NVMe space for the expert store (GLM ~95 GB, Flash-Next ~23 GB) on a filesystem where O_DIRECT works (on btrfs, a `chattr +C` directory). ```sh git clone --recursive https://github.com/horvay/b70-inference && cd b70-inference ./b70 setup # once, CPU only: the image, kernels and plugins for every model ./b70 prepare /path/to/glm-model # once per GLM model: pack its NVMe expert store (Flash-Next fills its own) ./b70 serve /path/to/model --port 8000 # OpenAI-compatible server; Ctrl-C or `./b70 stop` stops it ``` `b70 serve` picks the server from the model's `config.json` (`gemma4`, `qwen4_exp` or `glm5_next`) and options: `--ctx` (context tokens), `--parallel` (requests at once; GLM serves one), `--name` (model name in the API), `--drafter` (Gemma: its assistant model), `--store` (expert store directory), `--chat-template` (a template to use instead of the model's own), `--prepare` (run `prepare` first if needed). `./b70 info <model>` says whether setup and prepare are done. The requested port opens only after one real warm-up request, so a client never meets a server that is up but still compiling. `serve` also guards the machine: it refuses to start with too little free RAM, stops the server at the first `xe` fault, hang or reset for the B70 in the kernel log (faulting kernels that keep running have wedged the driver), when RAM runs low or memory pressure gets high, or when another GPU job (`B70_OTHER_JOBS`, a regex over command lines; sd-cli, ComfyUI, llama-server and Fish Speech by default) opens the B70. The server runs with `oom_score_adj` 1000, so the OOM killer takes it first. State (image, overlays, caches, stores, logs) lives in `state/` (`B70_STATE` elsewhere; `B70_ROOTFS` reuses an image already unpacked). ## Layout - `b70`: the command above. - `common/`: the image puller (`oci_pull.py`, by digest, resumable, OCI whiteouts) and the image's environment. - `glm/`: GLM-5.3-Flash on exllamav3 (submodule [horvay/exllamav3](https://github.com/horvay/exllamav3) `b70-xpu`) with the three-tier expert cache, SYCL/ESIMD and Triton kernels, and its server; history from [horvay/b70-glm53-flash](https://github.com/horvay/b70-glm53-flash). - `trellis-serve.patch`: everything changed in trellis-serve (SGLang's EXL3 plugin and kernels) for Gemma and Flash-Next. - `flashnext/`: the VRAM and RAM expert lists, and tools to rebuild them from your own workload. - `gemma/`: notes and measurements. # GLM-5.3-Flash on an Intel Arc Pro B70 GLM-5.3-Flash (a ~105 GB EXL3 mixture-of-experts model: 42 MoE layers × 288 experts, KDA linear attention, MLA/DSA sparse attention, mHC hyper-connections) running with one **Intel Arc Pro B70** (Battlemage, 32 GB) as the only GPU, on a desktop with 62 GB of RAM and one PCIe 4.0 NVMe SSD. The model does not fit in VRAM or RAM, so the routed experts are spread over three tiers: VRAM, pinned RAM, and an NVMe expert store. It started from [glm-flash-lite](https://github.com/sybil-solutions/glm-flash-lite) (which serves the model from a 24 GB RTX 3090 + RAM + NVMe on CUDA exllamav3) and [exllamav3](https://github.com/turboderp-org/exllamav3), and runs exllamav3's Python model code on PyTorch XPU with our own SYCL/ESIMD and Triton kernels underneath. Model used: [MikeRoz/GLM-5.3-Flash-Uncensored-2.51bpw-h6-exl3](https://huggingface.co/MikeRoz/GLM-5.3-Flash-Uncensored-2.51bpw-h6-exl3) (routed experts 2-bit in 22 layers, 3-bit in 20). **Result** (single user, greedy): | | First working version | Now | |---|---|---| | Decode | 5.2 tok/s | **~15-18 tok/s** | | Prompt, 2K tokens | ~40 s | **9.0 s** (225 tok/s) | | Prompt, 4K tokens | 72 s for 3.6K | **10.5 s** (382 tok/s) | | Prompt, 8K tokens | out of memory | **14.4 s** (556 tok/s), one pass | | Chat follow-up (2K cached + a short message) | re-read the whole history | **1.7-3 s** to the first token | ## Where the code is - `xpu/glm_xpu/`: the runtime. - `tier.py`: the three-tier expert cache (VRAM slots with frequency-gated admission, pinned RAM read zero-copy by the kernels over PCIe, NVMe store with parallel O_DIRECT reads; prompt staging). - `block_graphs.py`: per-block XPU graphs for decode, the no-sync router and its RAM margin, MTP verify steps. - `mla_graph.py`: MLA/DSA attention with device-side lengths so a whole layer fits in a graph. - `k_hc.py`, `k_kda.py`, `sparse_mla.py`: Triton / oneDNN kernels (mHC mix, KDA step, sparse MLA). - `live.py`: live speed meter (`tail -f run/live.log`). - `xpu/csrc/`: SYCL/ESIMD kernels. `exl3_moe.n128.sycl` is the grouped EXL3 MoE kernel (GEMV for decode, DPAS for prompts) reading expert weights through a pointer table, so VRAM- and RAM-resident experts look the same to it. The EXL3 linear kernels started from [trellis-serve](https://github.com/0xSero/trellis-serve) (`xpu/TRELLIS_COMMIT`). - `xpu/exllamav3_ext/`: stands in for exllamav3's compiled CUDA extension on XPU. - `xpu/pack_store.py`: packs the routed experts into the NVMe store. - `xpu/devserver.py`, `xpu/dev.py`, `xpu/snip/`: a persistent dev server (model loaded once) and the measurement snippets every number below comes from. - **exllamav3 fork:** [horvay/exllamav3 `b70-xpu`](https://github.com/horvay/exllamav3/tree/b70-xpu), also as `patches/exllamav3.patch` against turboderp-org/exllamav3 `958ec93` (v1.5.1). - `docs/dev-notes.md`: the full working notes, including hardware facts and Level Zero pitfalls. ## The bottlenecks, in the order they were hit ### Decode | # | Symptom | Cause | Fix | Effect | | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | | 1 | ~5 tok/s | One host sync per MoE layer: the CPU had to read the router's picks and make those experts resident before the GPU could continue (42 times per token) | **No-sync routing**: the router only picks experts already in VRAM or RAM (an NVMe-only pick becomes the next-best resident one); cache bookkeeping runs once per token, one token late, overlapped with the dense layers | ~12-13 tok/s | | 2 | MLA attention 15.7 ms/token | ~70 separately launched ops per layer, host-bound; their grids depended on host-side lengths | Device-side lengths and fixed-size top-k selection, whole MLA layer captured in an XPU graph (`mla_graph.py`) | 8.9 ms/token | | 3 | ~15 ms/token in tiny ops | The mHC hyper-connection mix ran its 1.5 MB reduction in a single work-group at batch 1, twice per block | Split the reduction over 64 work-groups + a finishing kernel | 166 → 17 µs per call | | 4 | ~20 ms/token reading RAM experts | Every RAM-resident pick is streamed over PCIe (~27 GB/s vs ~450 GB/s VRAM) | **RAM margin** in the router: a RAM expert's score is lowered by 0.2 for ranking only, so near-ties go to VRAM experts | 12.9 → 17.8 tok/s at the same quality (below) | RAM margin sweep (top-1 agreement and KL against exact routing, teacher-forced over 192 positions; speed on 128-token generations): | Margin | Top-1 vs exact | KL | Decode | | --- | --- | --- | --- | | 0 (plain no-sync) | 92.7% | 0.072 | 12.9 tok/s | | 0.05 | 92.7% | 0.061 | 15.2 tok/s | | 0.1 | 90.6% | 0.083 | 17.0 tok/s | | **0.2 (default)** | **91.7%** | **0.085** | **17.8 tok/s** | | 0.3 | 88.5% | 0.135 | 18.7 tok/s | | 0.5 | 88.5% | 0.171 | 18.9 tok/s | | VRAM only | 77.1% | 0.524 | 21.2 tok/s | ### Prompts | # | Symptom | Cause | Fix | Effect | | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | | 5 | Slow prompts in half the layers | The DPAS (matrix engine) prompt kernel was 3-bit only; the 2-bit layers fell back to GEMV | Generalized the DPAS B-operand build to 2- and 4-bit | prompt kernel for every layer | | 6 | Every prompt fetched all experts twice | exllamav3 runs a separate forward for the last page to save a recurrent (KDA) checkpoint | Stash the checkpoint inside the single forward (the KDA layer splits itself at that position) | 2K prompt 16-20 s → 9 s | | 7 | Out of memory above ~3.5K tokens; 0.7 GiB per 1K tokens | KDA prefill kept dead fp32 tensors alive; MLA's sparse path held ~200 KB/token of per-row tensors; XPU SDPA materialized the scores (quadratic) | Free early, in-place cumsum, per-layer splits (KDA every 2048, MLA 1024-token sub-chunks, MLP row slices) | ~85 MiB per 1K tokens; 8K in one pass | | 8 | Crash on the first token after a long prompt | PyTorch kept the prompt's freed blocks reserved; the next graph replay got `OUT_OF_DEVICE_MEMORY` | Release the cache at the end of a large forward | fixed | | 9 | 18.5 s for a short chat follow-up | The prompt path streamed every non-resident expert of the layers ahead | Short prompts read only the experts they picked, in parallel | 1.7 s | ### MTP (speculative decoding): tried, not a win here The model ships an MTP head, and it works (68-88% of drafts accepted), with verify steps on the graph/no-sync path. But consecutive tokens share only ~1 of 8 experts per layer, and expert reads dominate the step, so checking 2 tokens costs about twice one token. At margin 0.2: MTP off ~15-18 tok/s, MTP 1 ~15-20, MTP 2 ~12-16, MTP 4 ~10. MTP pays off when the experts are in VRAM; with a 105 GB model on a 32 GB card it is off by default (`GLM_XPU_MTP`). ## Running it From the repo root (see the [top-level README](../README.md) for what the host needs): ```bash ./b70 setup glm # once: the shared image, glm/pylib, the SYCL kernels (CPU only) huggingface-cli download MikeRoz/GLM-5.3-Flash-Uncensored-2.51bpw-h6-exl3 --local-dir /path/to/glm53-model ./b70 prepare /path/to/glm53-model # once: pack the routed experts into the NVMe store (~95 GB written) ./b70 serve /path/to/glm53-model --port 8053 # OpenAI-compatible server (glm/xpu/serve.py), one request at a time ``` The server takes chat completions (streamed or not; the model's own chat template, tools, thinking as `reasoning_content`, `chat_template_kwargs.enable_thinking` and `reasoning_effort`) and raw completions. A chat follow-up that repeats the previous prompt reuses its cached pages and recurrent checkpoint. The first prompt after a start is slow (~60 s of one-time kernel compiling); expect the first decode right after a start to be slower too: the RAM tier fills in the background and the VRAM tier adapts to what the model actually uses. For development, `scripts/dev_restart.sh` starts a persistent dev server and `xpu/dev.py` runs a Python snippet inside it (`model`, `tok`, `gen` (exllamav3 Generator), `tier` are in scope; see `xpu/snip/`). `scripts/in_rootfs.sh` runs a command in the image with this folder at `/w` (`GLM53_MODELS`, `GLM53_NVX` set the model and store binds). Settings (environment): | Variable | Default | | | --- | --- | --- | | `GLM_XPU_RAM_MARGIN` | 0.2 | router margin against RAM experts (0 = plain no-sync) | | `GLM_XPU_NOSYNC` | 1 | 0 = exact routing (one host sync per MoE layer, ~8.7 tok/s) | | `GLM_XPU_MTP` | 0 | MTP draft tokens | | `GLM_XPU_CHUNK` | 8192 | prompt tokens per forward | | `GLM_XPU_MLA_GRAPH` | 1 | MLA attention inside the decode graphs | ## Known limits - The first prompt after a server start takes ~60 s (one-time JIT compile of the SYCL kernels). - Prompts over 8K tokens run in 8K pieces, each fetching the experts again; 12K in one pass runs out of VRAM. - Batch size 1: one request at a time. - Faults seen while developing (all fixed, see `docs/dev-notes.md`): a failed XPU graph capture leaves PyTorch's graph pool recording and later replays fault the GPU, so a capture failure now disables graphs for the session; multi-row `topk`/`sort` touched the pinned host allocator during capture. ## License MIT (see `LICENSE`). Third-party credits in `NOTICE`. # glm-b70: GLM-5.3-Flash (glm-flash-lite) with the Arc Pro B70 as the primary GPU glm-flash-lite (sybil-solutions) serves GLM-5.3-Flash EXL3 3.05 bpw (125.3 GB) from one 24 GB RTX 3090 + RAM + an NVMe expert store, on stock exllamav3 1.5.1 (CUDA). This workspace ports it so the 32 GB Intel Arc Pro B70 runs the model. Since 2026-10-10 the model is MikeRoz/GLM-5.3-Flash-Uncensored-2.51bpw-h6-exl3 (`d6eb7f51`, 105 GB, abliterated): routed experts are K=2 in 22 layers and K=3 in 20 (+ MTP), so store records, cache slot pools and kernel K are per layer. No Docker: everything runs in trellis-b70's unpacked SGLang XPU rootfs (torch 2.13 XPU, Triton XPU, oneAPI 2026.0) through bwrap, with a tmp overlay so the rootfs itself is never written. ## Layout | path | what | |---|---| | `exllamav3/` | turboderp exllamav3 at v1.5.1 (`958ec93`), branch `xpu`: Python model code, CUDA calls mapped to XPU | | `xpu/exllamav3_ext/` | the XPU shim that stands in for exllamav3's compiled CUDA extension (torch / Triton / ESIMD) | | `xpu/exl3xpu/`, `xpu/csrc/` | exl3xpu ESIMD kernels vendored from trellis-serve (`xpu/TRELLIS_COMMIT`) + the GLM MoE kernel (`exl3_moe.n128.sycl` from glm-flash-lite b70tier) | | `xpu/build.sh` | builds `exl3xpu/_C.so` (EXL3 linear) and `exl3xpu/_moe_glm.so` (grouped MoE, swiglu clamp 10) | | `xpu/pack_store.py` | packs the routed experts into the NVMe store in exl3xpu blob order | | `glm-flash-lite/` | upstream repo, branch `b70-primary` | | `moetier/` | upstream campaign notes (B70 microbench, latency analyses), reference only | | `scripts/in_rootfs.sh` | `in_rootfs.sh [--gpu] cmd...`: workspace at `/w`, weights ro at `/models`, store at `/nvx` | ## Data - Weights: `/mnt/sandisk/glm53/model` (MikeRoz 2.51 bpw uncensored, 105 GB, SATA SSD; read at load and by the packer only). The 3.05 bpw turboderp weights and their store were deleted on 2026-10-10. The MTP layer (model layer 45) is in the main shards. - Expert store: `~/models/glm53-store/glm53_flash_exl3_experts_blob.{bin,json}` (format v2: per-layer K, record size, offset; 990 PRO, btrfs dir with `chattr +C` so O_DIRECT is not defeated by zstd compression), ~95 GB. ## Status (2026-10-10) Correct: reference panel top-1 0.987 / KL 0.0037 vs stock exllamav3 on CUDA. Decode ~5.2 tok/s exact (256-token greedy, 27-30 GB RAM tier). Prefill being rebuilt (streaming stager, DPAS MoE re-enabled). | piece | how it runs on the B70 | |---|---| | EXL3 linears | exl3xpu ESIMD (`exl3_gemm_small` / `linear`) | | routed experts | `glm_xpu/tier.py`: VRAM slots (frequency-gated admission) + pinned RAM tier read zero-copy by the MoE kernel + NVMe store; layer-ahead prefetch; exclusive tiers with a VRAM victim ring; prefill streams non-resident experts through a pinned ring | | MoE kernel | `csrc/exl3_moe.n128.sycl`: grouped GEMV for decode, DPAS for M >= 256 (gate/up work-group split to <= 32 threads: 64 hung the B70 in large-GRF mode) | | KDA | decode step `glm_xpu/k_kda.py` (Triton); prefill vendored fla chunk_kda (Triton) | | MLA | sparse (DSA) attention `glm_xpu/sparse_mla.py` (gather + oneDNN bmm, 5-22x the Triton kernel); dense prefill torch SDPA (40-90x) | | mHC, norms | `glm_xpu/k_hc.py` (Triton) | | decode step | `glm_xpu/block_graphs.py`: XPU graphs per block around the one host sync per MoE layer | Dev loop: `scripts/dev_restart.sh` starts `xpu/devserver.py` (model loaded once, kernel modules hot-reload); `python3 xpu/dev.py xpu/snip/<snippet>.py` runs a snippet on the live model. Kernel unit tests: `xpu/tests/kernels.py`, `xpu/tests/units.py`. Router substitution ("dynamic REAP", `GLM_XPU_SUBST_KEEP`) exists but is off: ~7x KL for <10%. Measured 2026-10-10 (decode timed without the prompt): no-sync 12.3-13.2 tok/s, exact 8.7 (no-sync per token: routed experts 30.7 ms, of which RAM-tier experts over PCIe ~20; MLA attention eager 15.7; phase A 16.3; Bn 10.6; bookkeeping 4.1). MTP spec decode runs (~5 tok/s; the multi-token verify forward is eager and exact). Short prompts go through ensure() (admit_max 512). Prompts are chunked at 2048 tokens: prompt activations grow ~0.7 GiB per 1K tokens and 8192 ran the B70 out of memory (Level Zero segfault) - cause not found yet. Measured 2026-10-11 (all run on the B70): - DPAS prefill for K=2/K=4 (`build4_any`): `units.py --moe` rel err 7.2e-4 at M=300 for K=2, 3, 4 (same as K=3 before). - MLA decode in the phase-A graph (`mla_graph.py`, phase "Am"): vs `MLAttention.forward` on the same state, rel err 1.2e-4 at 2.6K context (sparse), 1.4e-3 short (eager uses dense attention there). MLA per token 15.7 -> 8.9 ms. Short-context decode 15.4-15.9 tok/s with a warm expert cache (12-13.4 right after a restart: ~20 NVMe reads/token). - Prompt memory: KDA prefill held dead fp32 tensors (projection, gate temporaries, fla input_guard copies, the cumsum copy): per-layer peak 388 -> 208 MiB per 1K tokens, values bit-identical (`snip/kda_eq.py`). Whole prompt: 2K tokens 1.00 -> 0.59 GiB extra; 4K tokens 1.83 GiB. The crash after a long prompt was the first decode graph replay getting OUT_OF_DEVICE_MEMORY (torch kept the prompt's blocks cached): `_end_large_forward` now runs at the first block of the next step and empties the cache. Prompt chunk 2048 -> 4096. - Prompt speed: 2K tokens 16.5 s (122 tok/s), 3.3K 17.2 s, 4K 17.9 s (was 72 s for 3.6K). The first prompt after a server start is slow (Triton compile/autotune: 80 s at 4K). Most remaining time is the MoE layers waiting on RAM/NVMe expert reads. - Staging refill after a prompt: "refilled 288 staging slots ... in 0.4-0.7 s". Prefill, later 2026-10-11: - One pass per prompt: the recurrent checkpoint at the last full page is stashed inside the forward (GatedDeltaNet splits at `recurrent_ckpt_offset`, job.py `_put_inpass_checkpoint`) instead of exllamav3's separate last-page forward, which fetched every routed expert a second time. Checkpoint differs from the old one by the same rounding as changing the chunk size (`snip/inpass_ref.py`). 2K prompt 16-20 s -> 9.0 s. - Per-layer activation bounds (KDA split every 2048, MLA sequential 1024-token sub-chunks, dense MLP row slices, MLA dense prefill in head groups / query blocks): ~85 MiB per 1K prompt tokens. Chunk 8192: 4K 10.5 s, 6K 13.2 s, 8K 14.4 s (556 tok/s), peak 1.46 GiB extra; 12K runs out of device memory (and leaves the context broken: kill the server). - Chat follow-up (2K cached + ~40 new tokens): prompts under 2048 picks read only their picked experts in parallel (tier `pf_min_picks`) instead of streaming every non-resident expert ahead; prompts over 8 tokens take the staging path (`admit_max` 64). First token 18.5 s -> 1.7 s (38 new tokens), 3.1 s (59). - First prompt after a server start is still ~60 s (one-time kernel JIT: SYCL kernels are spir64, not AOT; SYCL_CACHE_PERSISTENT crashed the server in libc, NEO_CACHE_PERSISTENT had no effect). ## Next 1. Prefill: streaming stager (written, untested), then measure 4K / 16K prompts. 2. MTP speculative decoding (checkpoint ships `mtp.safetensors`; GLM-5.3 MTP acceptance ~70-75% on real text). 3. A real OpenAI-compatible server (glm-flash-lite `glm53/serve.py` on this runner) for chat clients. 4. Later: sglang Intel `chunk_kda`, the 3080 Ti as an extra expert tier. ## Hardware facts - B70 sits behind its own PCIe switch on a PCIe 4.0 x16 root port (~24-27 GB/s H2D). RTX 3080 Ti: PCIe 4.0 x4. - Ryzen 9 5900XT, 32 threads, 62 GiB RAM shared with the desktop. 990 PRO (PCIe 4.0 x4) under LUKS + btrfs: ~5.5 GB/s O_DIRECT with 16 readers x 8 pieces per record, ~2 ms per 9.47 MB record. - Level Zero pitfalls hit here: O_DIRECT into USM host memory -> EFAULT; one pinned allocation > ~4 GB silently returns a null pointer; torch's pinned allocator rounds to powers of two (use 226-record chunks); interpreter teardown with ~30 GB pinned can spin for 15+ minutes (devserver exits with os._exit). # exllamav3_ext → XPU porting spec for GLM-5.3-Flash (ext op inventory) Target: exllamav3 **v1.5.1** (commit `958ec93`, checkout `glm/exllamav3`) running GLM-5.3-Flash EXL3 (`turboderp/GLM-5.3-Flash-exl3`, rev `332ab457`, 3.05 bpw) on one Intel Arc Pro B70. The plan replaces the compiled `exllamav3_ext` module with a Python shim built from torch, Triton and the existing XPU kernels. Text-only, single device, no tensor parallel, driven by `glm/glm-flash-lite/glm53/serve.py` (`-cs 131072 --max-batch-size 8 -chunk_size 8192 -ambs 4`, AsyncGenerator, no `--mtp`/`-dm`/`-ngram`/`-cq`). Conventions: - Paths are relative to `glm/exllamav3`. The Python package is `exllamav3/` and the CUDA sources are `exllamav3/exllamav3_ext/`. Most per-part tables drop the `exllamav3/` prefix; each part states its base. - Line numbers are for v1.5.1. - "Shim" means the replacement `exllamav3_ext` Python module. - Checkpoint facts come from the safetensors headers of all 15 shards, read with HTTP range requests (no weights downloaded), and from `/mnt/sandisk/glm53/model/config.json` and `kpool_aux.safetensors` (read-only). Layout of this document: 1. Direct answers: MTP, codebook, batch sizes. 2. GLM5Next forward at a high level, and the state each layer keeps. 3. Recommended XPU configuration: env vars, flags, Python patches, shim rules. 4. Master op table in hot-path order, then the ops the configuration avoids and the ops never reached. 5. Per-op detail, Parts A–F: signatures, exact math, data layouts, call sites, CUDA deps and Triton kernels per area. - Part A: EXL3/FP16 linear and the quant kernels (trellis layout, mul1 decode, Hadamard). - Part B: dense MLP, MoE, routing, activations. - Part C: KDA linear attention and recurrent state, plus the vendored fla Triton kernels. - Part D: MLA (NoPE) and the k-pool DSA indexer, the MLA/DSA Triton kernels, and the KV/indexer cache. - Part E: hyper-connections, RMSNorm, TransformerBlock, model forward, load, warmup, loader, graphs. - Part F: generator, page table, sampling, cache allocation, recurrent checkpoints. 6. Consolidated non-ext CUDA dependencies and Triton kernel summary. 7. Minimal op set for a correct (not fast) forward. --- ## 1. Direct answers **Is MTP used by default? No.** - `model_init.init` turns MTP on only with `--mtp` or `-dm <model_dir>` (`exllamav3/model_init.py:197-204`), and serve.py's documented command passes neither. - `--ngram_match_min` defaults to 0, so `num_draft_tokens = 0` and `max_history = 0` (`model_init.py:270-275`, `generator/generator.py:168-171`). - As a result `recurrent_history` is always False, and the rewind ops are not reached (`batched_conv_rewind`/`batched_state_rewind`). - **The checkpoint has no MTP layer at all.** `layers.45.*` is absent even though `num_nextn_predict_layers = 1`. `--mtp` would first need a `convert_mtp.py` output placed next to the model. - `architecture/glm5_next_mtp.py` is off the path. Skip it. **Which codebook and bitrates?** - **mul1 everywhere.** Every EXL3 linear ships a scalar I32 `<key>.mul1` (value `0x83DCD12D`, a flag only; the multiplier is hard-coded in the kernels). There are zero `.mcg` tensors, no bias, and no packed `su`/`sv` sign tensors, only `suh`/`svh` fp16. - Bitrates are whole numbers, so the fractional-trellis (`frac.cu`, half-integer K) code is never reached. The checkpoint was produced by exllamav3 1.4.4; fractional support landed after 1.5.0. | tensors | K | |---|---| | routed experts | 3 | | dense MLP (layers 0–2) | 4 | | shared experts, every attention/KDA projection, indexer `wq_b` | 5 | | `lm_head` (`head_bits`) | 6 | This matches the K set the existing trellis-serve XPU kernels support. Details in Part A §1.0. **Decode batch sizes.** - serve.py's `-ambs 4` is passed by `model_init` as the Cache's `max_batch_size`, which sets `Cache.num_slots = 4` recurrent-state slots. - For recurrent models the Generator then clamps its batch to `min(max_batch_size, cache.num_slots)` (`generator/generator.py:235`). So **decode runs at bsz 1–4 (q_len 1)** despite `--max-batch-size 8`. - The ops must still accept up to 8 rows: warmup and `MAX_BSZN = 8` buffers use 8, and `-ambs 8` would make bsz 5–8 real. - Prefill is always batch 1, in chunks of up to 8192 tokens. --- ## 2. GLM5Next forward at a high level Config: hidden 4096, 45 layers, vocab 154880, rms_norm_eps 1e-5. Module list from `architecture/glm5_next.py:149-344`: | idx | module | notes | |---|---|---| | 0 | `Embedding` | `prefer_cpu`: lives on the **CPU**, bf16/fp16 table, output **fp32** (b,s,4096); uploaded through a pinned buffer at decode | | 1 | `ExpandStreams` | `x.float().unsqueeze(2).expand(..., 4, ...)`: the residual becomes **4 identical fp32 streams** (b,s,4,4096) | | 2..46 | 45 × `TransformerBlock` | below | | 47 | `HyperHead(mean=True)` | `x.mean(dim=2)`, no weights (`hc_head` op not used) | | 48 | `RMSNorm` (final) | fp32 in, fp16 out | | 49 | `Linear lm_head` | EXL3 K=6, fp16 logits; the generator slices to the last token first (`last_tokens_only`) | **Per-layer structure.** `modules/transformer.py:137-215` is the same for every layer. Only the attention and MLP kinds differ. ``` post, comb, y = hc_mix(streams) # mHC "pre": y = Σ_h pre_h·stream_h (fp16), plus post (4), comb (4x4) y = input_layernorm(y) # RMSNorm fp16 -> fp16 y = attention(y) # KDA (34 layers) or MLA+indexer (11 layers); out fp32 streams = post ⊗ y + combᵀ · streams # hc_apply, in place, fp32 post, comb, y = hc_mix(streams) # second HC site (ffn) y = post_attention_layernorm(y) y = mlp(y) # dense GatedMLP (layers 0-2) or MoE (3-44); out fp32 streams = post ⊗ y + combᵀ · streams ``` For non-final prefill chunks, `Model.prefill` stops after the **attention** of the last block. That fills the caches; HyperHead, the norm and lm_head do not run (`transformer.py:160`, `model_ls.py:298-314`). **Hyper-connections** (mHC, `hc_mult = 4`, Sinkhorn 20 iterations, `hc_eps = 1e-6`). Each layer has two sites, `hc_attn` and `hc_ffn`. Each site has `fn` (24,16384) fp32, `base` (24) and `scale` (3). ``` mix = streams_flat(16384) · fnᵀ ; r = rsqrt(mean(streams²) + 1e-5) pre = σ(mix[0:4]·r·scale0 + base) + 1e-6 post = 2σ(mix[4:8]·r·scale1 + base) comb = Sinkhorn(softmax_j(mix[8:24]·r·scale2 + base) + eps) ``` Sinkhorn is one column normalisation followed by 19 (row, column) normalisation pairs, each adding eps. Exact kernel math is in Part E. The HC streams carry **no cache state**. **Layer kinds.** `layer_types` puts MLA at 3, 7, 11, …, 43 (11 layers) and KDA at the other 34 (3:1). `mlp_layer_types` makes layers 0, 1 and 2 dense and 3–44 MoE (`first_k_dense_replace = 3`; layer 44 is sparse). Every `indexer_types` entry is `"full"`. **KDA (Kimi delta attention) layers**: `GatedDeltaNet` in KDA mode, 64 heads, Dk = Dv = 128. 1. `qkv = qkv_proj(x)` (EXL3, 4096 → 24576). 2. Depthwise causal conv (K=4) with SiLU over the 24576 channels, using the per-slot conv state. 3. q and k are L2-normalised (eps 1e-6). 4. Per-channel log-decay `g = −5·σ(exp(A_log[h])·(f_b(f_a(x)) + dt_bias))`, from low-rank f_a/f_b fp16 linears (`gate_lower_bound = −5`). 5. `β = σ(b_proj(x))`. 6. Delta rule per token: `S = diag(e^g)·S; u = v − Sᵀk; S += β·k·uᵀ; o = Sᵀq/√128`. 7. Gated RMSNorm per head (eps 1e-5, weight `o_norm` (128), gate `σ(g_b(g_a(x)))`). 8. `o_proj` (EXL3, 8192 → 4096, fp32 out). Prefill of 64 tokens or more uses the vendored fla `chunk_kda` Triton kernels. Shorter prefills and decode use the recurrent kernel. Cache state per layer per slot: | tensor | dtype | shape | |---|---|---| | `recurrent_state` | fp32 | (slots, 1, 64, 128, 128), 4 MiB per slot, `[slot, hist, h, k, v]` with v contiguous | | `conv_state` | bf16 | (slots, 24576, 4) | Host-RAM checkpoints of these states are taken every 2048 tokens (32768 during long prompts) into the 4 GiB `RecurrentCache`. This is pure torch copying (Part C, Part F). **MLA layers**: NoPE (`qk_rope_head_dim = 0`), 64 heads, `kv_lora_rank` 512, `q_lora_rank` 1536, qk 256, v 256, `sm_scale = 1/16`. 1. `q = q_b(RMSNorm(q_a(x)))` → (R,64,256). 2. The latent `ckv = RMSNorm(kv_a(x))` (512) is **the only per-token KV cached**: fp16, paged at 256 tokens per page. 3. `kv_b_proj` (fp16, unquantized) is split at load into `W_UK` and `W_UV`. Decode and short chunks use the absorbed form (`q_lat = q·W_UK`, attention over the latent, `o = o_lat·W_UV`). Dense prefill chunks over 16 tokens use the MHA form. 4. `o_proj` (16384 → 4096, fp32 out). **k-pool DSA indexer** (32 heads × 128, `index_topk` 2048, `index_kpool` 4, `always_select_tail`): - **Every** forward computes indexer keys `k = LayerNorm(wk·x)` (eps 1e-6, no rope) and gate scores `gate_w·x` (128; from `kpool_aux.safetensors`) and caches them. - Each completed group of 4 tokens is pooled: `pool_k[d] = Σ_j softmax_j(gate[j,d] + ape[j,d])·k[j,d]`. - Once visible context exceeds 2048 tokens (the "sparse" regime, which already includes the first 8192-token prefill chunk), each query row: - scores pools with `(1/√(128·32))·Σ_h w_h·relu(q_idx,h·pool_k)`, where `q_idx = wq_b(q_resid)` and `w = weights_proj(x)`; - takes the top 512 pools (`dsa_topk`) and expands each to its 4 tokens, plus the raw tokens of its own incomplete tail pool. That is ≤ 2051 token indices, padded to 2080; - attends only to those latent rows. Cache state per MLA layer (fp16, 512 pages at `-cs 131072`, 1600 B/token in total): | tensor | shape | contents | |---|---|---| | `k` | (pages,256,1,512) | latent | | `v` | (pages,256,1,0) | empty (NoPE) | | `k_idx` | (pages,256,256) | indexer key ‖ gate | | `k_pool` | (pages,64,128) | pooled keys | There is no recurrent state (Part D). **MLP layers**: - **Dense (layers 0–2):** `GatedMLP` 4096 → 12288 → 4096. - **MoE (layers 3–44):** 288 routed experts (I = 2048) + 1 shared expert (I = 2048), top-8. - **Activation** in both: `a = min(silu(g), 10)·clamp(u, −10, 10)` (`swiglu_limit = 10`; the gate is clamped *after* SiLU, from above only). - **Routing** (`router_type "dots"` → `routing_ds3_nogroup`), sigmoid / noaux_tc, `n_group = 1`: 1. `logits = fp16(x·gate)`. 2. `s = σ(fp32(logits))`. 3. Select the top-8 by `s + e_score_correction_bias`. The bias is used for selection only. 4. Weights `w = s·2.5/(Σ_sel s + 1e-20)` (`norm_topk_prob`, `routed_scaling_factor = 2.5`), stored fp16. - **Output** = `Σ_k w_k·down_k(act(gate_k x, up_k x))` + `shared(x)`, accumulated in fp32, with no shared gate (Part B). --- ## 3. Recommended XPU configuration (correct first, fast later) **Env vars and flags** | setting | why | |---|---| | `EXL3_BC_GDN=0` | KDA layers use the torch path. Avoids `BC_GatedDeltaNetSplit` (CUDA-graph fused layer), `kda_gate_op`, `gdn_ba_gemv`, `gdn_lowrank_gemv_f` (`modules/gated_delta_net.py:20`) | | `EXL3_BC_ATTN=0` | MLA uses the eager Triton path. Avoids `BC_MLAttention`, `TritonKernel` and the cubin AOT compile. It covers `EXL3_BC_MLA` **and** the load-time `autosplit_extra_measure → build_bc_mla` call that `EXL3_BC_MLA=0` alone misses (`modules/attention_fn/bc_attn.py:42`, `modules/mla_attn.py:1110-1118`) | | `config.infer_params.no_reconstruct = True` (set between `Config.from_directory` and `model.load`, e.g. patch `model_init.py` around :194) | Every EXL3 `Linear` goes through `BC_LinearEXL3.run_alloc` at every row count (`modules/quant/exl3.py:140`). The MoE uses the torch per-expert loop at every batch size (`block_sparse_mlp.py:1052-1055`), and no `MultiLinear`/`exl3_mgemm`/`BC_BlockSparseMLP`/fused-MoE buffers are built (`block_sparse_mlp.py:527,533,612`, `mlp.py:635`). This removes `exl3_mgemm` and its raw-pointer tables entirely | | `TRITON_F32_DEFAULT=ieee` | The fp32 `tl.dot` in `chunk_kda_fwd_kernel_inter_solve_fused` has no explicit precision (Part C (d)) | | `-nw` during bring-up | Skips warmup, which only pays Triton/autotune/graph first-use costs. Re-enable later to pre-JIT | | leave `GLM53_*` unset | All glm-flash-lite monkeypatches (`k_hcfuse` inline CUDA, `triton_pin` NVIDIA autotune picks, the tiers) are CUDA-specific | | no `-cq`, no `--mtp`, `-ccs 0` | Keeps the quantized-cache, draft and CPU page-cache ops unreached | | `EXL3_FUSED_SAMPLER` default (on) | The whole sampler collapses to one `fused_sampler` call (Part F) | **Python patches the shim cannot cover.** Each one is a hard failure or a silent wrong path on XPU. | file:line | problem | fix | |---|---|---| | `model_init.py:360` / `model/model.py:551` / `model/model_ls.py:150` | autosplit uses `torch.cuda.device_count()` and int device ordinals (= CUDA). It also runs an 8192-token dummy forward per module at load and calls `torch.cuda` memory APIs (`util/memory.py`) | load single-device: `model.load(device=torch.device("xpu", 0))` (`model_init.init(args, device=...)` forwards kwargs) → `_load_single`, with no dummy forward and no memory accounting | | `loader/safetensors.py:413` (default `load_method="mt_fread"`), `:793` | the stloader routes jobs on `tensor.is_cuda`, so an XPU tensor goes down the CPU path and the host writes through a device pointer | `load_method="python"` (`:839-857`): no ext calls at all. `Config.from_directory` passes `kwargs.get("load_method")` (`model/config.py:132`); patch `model_init.py:207` or the default | | `modules/block_sparse_mlp.py:818` | `load_local()`/`load_routing()` run only if `device.type == "cuda"`. On XPU `routing_cfg` stays None and the first MoE forward crashes | accept `"xpu"` | | `modules/gated_delta_net_fn/conv1d.py:245`, `:279` | the Triton conv raises `if not x.is_cuda`, and launches under `torch.cuda.device` | device-type check; `torch.xpu.device` | | `modules/gated_delta_net_fn/conv1d.py:386` | `is_cuda` gate for the CUDA conv op | leave it: on XPU it routes every conv to Triton, so `cuda_causal_conv1d_update` is never needed | | `vendor/fla/utils.py:117` (`input_guard`) | `torch.cuda.device(idx)` on every `chunk_kda` | `torch.xpu.device` | | `modules/attention_fn/mla_triton.py:803,823,865,977,1029,1045,1127,1210`, `dsa_triton.py:1052,1082,1153` | `with torch.cuda.device(...)` around launches | `torch.xpu.device` (or a no-op context) | | `modules/attention_fn/mla_triton.py:786` | `torch.cuda.get_device_properties(i).multi_processor_count` for split heuristics | XPU equivalent (Xe-core count, e.g. `gpu_subslice_count`; verify the field name in the installed torch) | | `generator/generator.py:1128` | `torch.cuda.synchronize(batch_logits.device)` **every decode step** (also the barrier that makes reusing pinned staging buffers safe) | `torch.xpu.synchronize(dev)`. Do not drop it | | `generator/generator.py:1122` | `next_token.is_cuda` gates the batched pinned read-back | `device.type != "cpu"` (otherwise correct, just slower) | | `util/misc.py:35-43` (`cuda_sync_active`) | becomes a silent no-op on XPU (first-token timing only) | iterate XPU devices | | `pin_memory=True` sites (`generator.py:938,1113-1114`, `job.py:1537`, `modules/embedding.py:164`) | XPU-pinned host memory | verify `.is_pinned()` on the installed torch-xpu. Fall back to unpinned (synchronous, still correct) | **Shim construction rules** - **Export every name in `bindings.cpp`.** Most only need to raise on call (e.g. a module `__getattr__` that returns a raising stub). Some names are touched at import or construct time: - `ext.relu_mul` at import (`modules/moe_batch_recon.py:61`; dict built at `:55-61`); - `gelu_mul`/`relu2_mul`/`silu_oai_mul`/`xielu` in `match` statements (`mlp.py:593-596`, `block_sparse_mlp.py:392-411`); - the int constants `FUSED_SAMPLER_MAX_BLOCKS = 128` and `FUSED_SAMPLER_HIST_STRIDE = 24640`. - **`BC_LinearEXL3` must exist as a real Python class.** `LinearEXL3.__init__` constructs it unconditionally (`modules/quant/exl3.py:86`) and every quantized linear calls `self.bc.run_alloc`. It must expose the fields `trellis, suh, svh, K, bias, mcg, mul1` (other BC constructors read them). Likewise `BC_LinearFP16` (`modules/quant/fp16.py:54`) and `BC_GatedRMSNorm` (`modules/gated_rmsnorm.py:55`) are constructed unconditionally at load. - **`BC_GatedMLP` must also exist (as a small Python class) even with `no_reconstruct`.** `GatedMLP.load_local` builds it whenever `downs[0].inner.bc` exists and gate/up are EXL3 with equal width (`mlp.py:655-687`), which is always true once `BC_LinearEXL3` exists. `GatedMLP.forward` then calls `bc.run_bszN(x, d)` for rows ≤ 8 (`mlp.py:744`). This covers the 3 dense layers and the shared expert of every MoE layer. Its body is `g = gate.run_alloc(x); u = up.run_alloc(x); a = silu_mul(g, u, 10); down.run(a, d)` (Part B, `BC_GatedMLP`). Part B suggests omitting `BC_LinearEXL3` to avoid this, but that is not possible: see `exl3.py:86`. - **Shim functions must honour in-place outputs.** Many callers pass preallocated outputs and ignore the return value, and the caller reads the output tensor (examples: `hc_mix` → post/comb/collapsed, `rms_norm` → y, `routing_ds3_nogroup` → topk buffers, `fused_sampler` → out, `cuda_recurrent_gated_delta_rule` → state and out). - **No raw pointers.** Under the recommended config no reached op receives device-address tables (`exl3_mgemm` and the `*_ptrs` MoE tables are avoided by `no_reconstruct`; rewind jobs by having no drafts). If the stock paths are revived later, the shim needs a `data_ptr → tensor` registry (Part A §1.2.9). **Numerical reference.** To compare XPU output against a CUDA run of stock exllamav3: - run CUDA with `EXL3_INT8_GEMV=0` (the default int8-activation GEMV adds about 0.9 % RMS noise on mul1 decode, Part A §1.2.2); - run CUDA with `EXL3_BC_GDN=0`, because the BC KDA path rounds differently: f_a/g_a stay fp32 and it uses a bf16 dt_bias (Part C). --- ## 4.
